People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Sometimes I even answer before I catch myself and realize no one else heard anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities since you started hearing the voices?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 43 / chief_executive / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"In meetings or gallery events I’ll suddenly stand up, rearrange pens or name cards into patterns, repeat a phrase someone just said, or hold my hands in the air like I’m presenting something, even though I know afterward it made no sense for the room.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

I’ve done things that aren’t normal for me. In meetings, or even at gallery events, I’ll suddenly stand up and start moving pens or name cards into patterns. Sometimes I repeat a phrase someone just said, or I hold my hands up like I’m presenting something, even when that’s not what’s happening.\n\nAfterward I can see it didn’t make sense for the situation, but in the moment I just… do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep track of your thoughts when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Not really. When the voices aren’t happening, I think I follow myself okay. I might get distracted or stressed like anyone, but I haven’t noticed my thoughts getting hard to track on their own.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble making sense when you're talking to others, even when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, people sometimes look confused if I’m distracted, but when I’m not hearing anything, I usually feel like I can explain myself normally. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m not making sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you only do those unusual actions, like arranging pens or repeating phrases, when you're hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. It seems to happen when I’m hearing the voices or when I’m really caught up in them. Like in a meeting or at a gallery event, I’ll suddenly stand up and start rearranging pens or name cards into patterns, or I’ll repeat something someone just said. Sometimes I’ll put my hands up like I’m presenting, even though afterward I know it didn’t fit what was happening in the room.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"intent": "Determine the presence of disorganized thinking when not hearing voices", "question": "Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not logical or are disconnected from each other when you're not hearing any voices?"}

When the voices aren’t happening, I think I follow myself okay. It sounds like board members or staff talking about me through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off. They’ll say I’m ruining the company, and it sounds so clear that I sometimes answer them before I realize no one else heard anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep hearing people talking about me when no one seems to be there. Like through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s turned off. It’s usually board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company. It sounds so clear that sometimes I answer them before I realize nobody else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people talking about you through the walls or your phone are actually plotting against you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they’re trying to hurt me physically. It feels like they’re against me, like they’re trying to push me out or make me look incompetent. I can’t prove it, but when I hear it, it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or have trouble organizing your thoughts when talking to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have trouble expressing myself most of the time. Maybe when I’m stressed I pause or lose my place, but I haven’t really noticed my thoughts being disorganized when I’m talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the voices you hear are real?\n\nPatient: At the time, yes, they feel real. I hear them so clearly—like they’re coming through the wall or from my phone, even when it’s off. It’s usually board members or staff talking about me, saying I’m ruining the company. Sometimes I even answer before I catch myself and realize no one else heard anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities since you started hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ve done things that aren’t normal for me. In meetings, or even at gallery events, I’ll suddenly stand up and start moving pens or name cards into patterns. Sometimes I repeat a phrase someone just said, or I hold my hands up like I’m presenting something, even when that’s not what’s happening.\n\nAfterward I can see it didn’t make sense for the situation, but in the moment I just… do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep track of your thoughts when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Not really. When the voices aren’t happening, I think I follow myself okay. I might get distracted or stressed like anyone, but I haven’t noticed my thoughts getting hard to track on their own.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble making sense when you're talking to others, even when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, people sometimes look confused if I’m distracted, but when I’m not hearing anything, I usually feel like I can explain myself normally. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m not making sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you only do those unusual actions, like arranging pens or repeating phrases, when you're hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. It seems to happen when I’m hearing the voices or when I’m really caught up in them. Like in a meeting or at a gallery event, I’ll suddenly stand up and start rearranging pens or name cards into patterns, or I’ll repeat something someone just said. Sometimes I’ll put my hands up like I’m presenting, even though afterward I know it didn’t fit what was happening in the room.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not logical or are disconnected from each other when you're not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that happens. When I’m not hearing anything, my thoughts feel pretty normal to me. I might be tired or distracted, but I don’t usually feel like they’re disconnected or not logical.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the things the voices say about you are true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. When it’s happening, it can feel true, or at least hard not to believe. Afterward, I’m less sure. I try to tell myself it might just be the voices, but in the moment it gets confusing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you hear are trying to tell you something true about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I think so, at least when it’s happening. It sounds like board members or staff talking about me through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off. They’ll say I’m ruining the company, and it sounds so clear that I sometimes answer them before I realize no one else heard anything.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voices, how long does it take you to respond or react to what they're saying?\n\nPatient: Usually pretty fast. If it sounds like they’re talking about me through the conference room wall or even from my phone, I’ll answer almost right away—sometimes before I really stop and think. Then I realize no one else heard it, and I feel embarrassed or confused.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep hearing people talking about me when no one seems to be there. Like through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s turned off. It’s usually board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company. It sounds so clear that sometimes I answer them before I realize nobody else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people talking about you through the walls or your phone are actually plotting against you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they’re trying to hurt me physically. It feels like they’re against me, like they’re trying to push me out or make me look incompetent. I can’t prove it, but when I hear it, it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or have trouble organizing your thoughts when talking to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have trouble expressing myself most of the time. Maybe when I’m stressed I pause or lose my place, but I haven’t really noticed my thoughts being disorganized when I’m talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the voices you hear are real?\n\nPatient: At the time, yes, they feel real. I hear them so clearly—like they’re coming through the wall or from my phone, even when it’s off. It’s usually board members or staff talking about me, saying I’m ruining the company. Sometimes I even answer before I catch myself and realize no one else heard anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities since you started hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ve done things that aren’t normal for me. In meetings, or even at gallery events, I’ll suddenly stand up and start moving pens or name cards into patterns. Sometimes I repeat a phrase someone just said, or I hold my hands up like I’m presenting something, even when that’s not what’s happening.\n\nAfterward I can see it didn’t make sense for the situation, but in the moment I just… do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep track of your thoughts when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Not really. When the voices aren’t happening, I think I follow myself okay. I might get distracted or stressed like anyone, but I haven’t noticed my thoughts getting hard to track on their own.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble making sense when you're talking to others, even when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, people sometimes look confused if I’m distracted, but when I’m not hearing anything, I usually feel like I can explain myself normally. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m not making sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you only do those unusual actions, like arranging pens or repeating phrases, when you're hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. It seems to happen when I’m hearing the voices or when I’m really caught up in them. Like in a meeting or at a gallery event, I’ll suddenly stand up and start rearranging pens or name cards into patterns, or I’ll repeat something someone just said. Sometimes I’ll put my hands up like I’m presenting, even though afterward I know it didn’t fit what was happening in the room.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not logical or are disconnected from each other when you're not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that happens. When I’m not hearing anything, my thoughts feel pretty normal to me. I might be tired or distracted, but I don’t usually feel like they’re disconnected or not logical.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the things the voices say about you are true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. When it’s happening, it can feel true, or at least hard not to believe. Afterward, I’m less sure. I try to tell myself it might just be the voices, but in the moment it gets confusing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you hear are trying to tell you something true about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I think so, at least when it’s happening. It sounds like board members or staff talking about me through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off. They’ll say I’m ruining the company, and it sounds so clear that I sometimes answer them before I realize no one else heard anything.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voices, how long does it take you to respond or react to what they're saying?\n\nPatient: Usually pretty fast. If it sounds like they’re talking about me through the conference room wall or even from my phone, I’ll answer almost right away—sometimes before I really stop and think. Then I realize no one else heard it, and I feel embarrassed or confused.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific, like rearranging pens or repeating phrases?\n\nPatient: Not really like rearranging pens or repeating phrases. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep hearing people talking about me when no one seems to be there. Like through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s turned off. It’s usually board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company. It sounds so clear that sometimes I answer them before I realize nobody else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people talking about you through the walls or your phone are actually plotting against you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they’re trying to hurt me physically. It feels like they’re against me, like they’re trying to push me out or make me look incompetent. I can’t prove it, but when I hear it, it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or have trouble organizing your thoughts when talking to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have trouble expressing myself most of the time. Maybe when I’m stressed I pause or lose my place, but I haven’t really noticed my thoughts being disorganized when I’m talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the voices you hear are real?\n\nPatient: At the time, yes, they feel real. I hear them so clearly—like they’re coming through the wall or from my phone, even when it’s off. It’s usually board members or staff talking about me, saying I’m ruining the company. I’ve done things that aren’t normal for me. In meetings, or even at gallery events, I’ll suddenly stand up and start moving pens or name cards into patterns. Sometimes I repeat a phrase someone just said, or I hold my hands up like I’m presenting something, even when that’s not what’s happening.\n\nAfterward I can see it didn’t make sense for the situation, but in the moment I just… do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep track of your thoughts when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Not really. When the voices aren’t happening, I think I follow myself okay. I might get distracted or stressed like anyone, but I haven’t noticed my thoughts getting hard to track on their own.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble making sense when you're talking to others, even when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, people sometimes look confused if I’m distracted, but when I’m not hearing anything, I usually feel like I can explain myself normally. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m not making sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you only do those unusual actions, like arranging pens or repeating phrases, when you're hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. It seems to happen when I’m hearing the voices or when I’m really caught up in them. Like in a meeting or at a gallery event, I’ll suddenly stand up and start rearranging pens or name cards into patterns, or I’ll repeat something someone just said. Sometimes I’ll put my hands up like I’m presenting, even though afterward I know it didn’t fit what was happening in the room.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not logical or are disconnected from each other when you're not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that happens. When I’m not hearing anything, my thoughts feel pretty normal to me. I might be tired or distracted, but I don’t usually feel like they’re disconnected or not logical.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the things the voices say about you are true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. When it’s happening, it can feel true, or at least hard not to believe. Afterward, I’m less sure. I try to tell myself it might just be the voices, but in the moment it gets confusing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you hear are trying to tell you something true about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I think so, at least when it’s happening. It sounds like board members or staff talking about me through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off. They’ll say I’m ruining the company, and it sounds so clear that I sometimes answer them before I realize no one else heard anything.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voices, how long does it take you to respond or react to what they're saying?\n\nPatient: Usually pretty fast. If it sounds like they’re talking about me through the conference room wall or even from my phone, I’ll answer almost right away—sometimes before I really stop and think. Then I realize no one else heard it, and I feel embarrassed or confused.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific, like rearranging pens or repeating phrases?\n\nPatient: Not really like rearranging pens or repeating phrases. It’s more that I hear people talking about me—like board members or staff through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off—saying I’m ruining the company. It sounds so clear that I answer them before I realize nobody else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble making sense or feel like your thoughts are jumbled when you're talking to others and not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed my thoughts getting jumbled when I’m just talking to someone normally. If that happens, I don’t always catch it myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep hearing people talking about me when no one seems to be there. Like through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s turned off. It’s usually board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company. It sounds so clear that sometimes I answer them before I realize nobody else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people talking about you through the walls or your phone are actually plotting against you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they’re trying to hurt me physically. It feels like they’re against me, like they’re trying to push me out or make me look incompetent. I can’t prove it, but when I hear it, it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or have trouble organizing your thoughts when talking to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have trouble expressing myself most of the time. Maybe when I’m stressed I pause or lose my place, but I haven’t really noticed my thoughts being disorganized when I’m talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the voices you hear are real?\n\nPatient: At the time, yes, they feel real. I hear them so clearly—like they’re coming through the wall or from my phone, even when it’s off. It’s usually board members or staff talking about me, saying I’m ruining the company. Sometimes I even answer before I catch myself and realize no one else heard anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities since you started hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ve done things that aren’t normal for me. In meetings, or even at gallery events, I’ll suddenly stand up and start moving pens or name cards into patterns. Sometimes I repeat a phrase someone just said, or I hold my hands up like I’m presenting something, even when that’s not what’s happening.\n\nAfterward I can see it didn’t make sense for the situation, but in the moment I just… do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep track of your thoughts when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Not really. When the voices aren’t happening, I think I follow myself okay. I might get distracted or stressed like anyone, but I haven’t noticed my thoughts getting hard to track on their own.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble making sense when you're talking to others, even when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, people sometimes look confused if I’m distracted, but when I’m not hearing anything, I usually feel like I can explain myself normally. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m not making sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you only do those unusual actions, like arranging pens or repeating phrases, when you're hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. It seems to happen when I’m hearing the voices or when I’m really caught up in them. Like in a meeting or at a gallery event, I’ll suddenly stand up and start rearranging pens or name cards into patterns, or I’ll repeat something someone just said. Sometimes I’ll put my hands up like I’m presenting, even though afterward I know it didn’t fit what was happening in the room.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not logical or are disconnected from each other when you're not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that happens. When I’m not hearing anything, my thoughts feel pretty normal to me. I might be tired or distracted, but I don’t usually feel like they’re disconnected or not logical.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the things the voices say about you are true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. When it’s happening, it can feel true, or at least hard not to believe. Afterward, I’m less sure. I try to tell myself it might just be the voices, but in the moment it gets confusing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you hear are trying to tell you something true about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I think so, at least when it’s happening. It sounds like board members or staff talking about me through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off. They’ll say I’m ruining the company, and it sounds so clear that I sometimes answer them before I realize no one else heard anything.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voices, how long does it take you to respond or react to what they're saying?\n\nPatient: Usually pretty fast. If it sounds like they’re talking about me through the conference room wall or even from my phone, I’ll answer almost right away—sometimes before I really stop and think. Then I realize no one else heard it, and I feel embarrassed or confused.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific, like rearranging pens or repeating phrases?\n\nPatient: Not really like rearranging pens or repeating phrases. It’s more that I hear people talking about me—like board members or staff through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off—saying I’m ruining the company. It sounds so clear that I answer them before I realize nobody else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble making sense or feel like your thoughts are jumbled when you're talking to others and not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed my thoughts getting jumbled when I’m just talking to someone normally. If that happens, I don’t always catch it myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing those unusual actions, like arranging pens or repeating phrases, because the voices are telling you to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s because the voices tell me to do it. It’s more like, in meetings or gallery events, I’ll suddenly stand up or start moving pens or name cards into patterns, or I’ll repeat something someone just said. Sometimes I’ll hold my hands up like I’m presenting something. Afterward I know it didn’t make sense for the situation, and it’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing those unusual actions, like arranging pens or repeating phrases, as a direct reaction to what the voices are saying?\n\nPatient: No, not really as a direct reaction to the voices. It just sort of happens. Like in meetings or at gallery events, I might suddenly stand up, move pens or name cards into patterns, repeat something someone just said, or hold my hands up like I’m presenting. Afterward I can tell it didn’t make sense for the room, and I feel embarrassed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep hearing people talking about me when no one seems to be there. It sounds so clear that sometimes I answer them before I realize nobody else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people talking about you through the walls or your phone are actually plotting against you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they’re trying to hurt me physically. It feels like they’re against me, like they’re trying to push me out or make me look incompetent. I can’t prove it, but when I hear it, it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or have trouble organizing your thoughts when talking to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have trouble expressing myself most of the time. Maybe when I’m stressed I pause or lose my place, but I haven’t really noticed my thoughts being disorganized when I’m talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the voices you hear are real?\n\nPatient: At the time, yes, they feel real. I hear them so clearly—like they’re coming through the wall or from my phone, even when it’s off. It’s usually board members or staff talking about me, saying I’m ruining the company. Sometimes I even answer before I catch myself and realize no one else heard anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities since you started hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ve done things that aren’t normal for me. In meetings, or even at gallery events, I’ll suddenly stand up and start moving pens or name cards into patterns. Sometimes I repeat a phrase someone just said, or I hold my hands up like I’m presenting something, even when that’s not what’s happening.\n\nAfterward I can see it didn’t make sense for the situation, but in the moment I just… do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep track of your thoughts when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Not really. When the voices aren’t happening, I think I follow myself okay. I might get distracted or stressed like anyone, but I haven’t noticed my thoughts getting hard to track on their own.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble making sense when you're talking to others, even when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, people sometimes look confused if I’m distracted, but when I’m not hearing anything, I usually feel like I can explain myself normally. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m not making sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you only do those unusual actions, like arranging pens or repeating phrases, when you're hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. It seems to happen when I’m hearing the voices or when I’m really caught up in them. Like in a meeting or at a gallery event, I’ll suddenly stand up and start rearranging pens or name cards into patterns, or I’ll repeat something someone just said. Sometimes I’ll put my hands up like I’m presenting, even though afterward I know it didn’t fit what was happening in the room.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not logical or are disconnected from each other when you're not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that happens. When I’m not hearing anything, my thoughts feel pretty normal to me. I might be tired or distracted, but I don’t usually feel like they’re disconnected or not logical.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the things the voices say about you are true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. When it’s happening, it can feel true, or at least hard not to believe. Afterward, I’m less sure. I try to tell myself it might just be the voices, but in the moment it gets confusing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you hear are trying to tell you something true about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I think so, at least when it’s happening. It sounds like board members or staff talking about me through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off. They’ll say I’m ruining the company, and it sounds so clear that I sometimes answer them before I realize no one else heard anything.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voices, how long does it take you to respond or react to what they're saying?\n\nPatient: Usually pretty fast. If it sounds like they’re talking about me through the conference room wall or even from my phone, I’ll answer almost right away—sometimes before I really stop and think. Then I realize no one else heard it, and I feel embarrassed or confused.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific, like rearranging pens or repeating phrases?\n\nPatient: Not really like rearranging pens or repeating phrases. It’s more that I hear people talking about me—like board members or staff through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off—saying I’m ruining the company. It sounds so clear that I answer them before I realize nobody else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble making sense or feel like your thoughts are jumbled when you're talking to others and not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed my thoughts getting jumbled when I’m just talking to someone normally. If that happens, I don’t always catch it myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing those unusual actions, like arranging pens or repeating phrases, because the voices are telling you to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s because the voices tell me to do it. It’s more like, in meetings or gallery events, I’ll suddenly stand up or start moving pens or name cards into patterns, or I’ll repeat something someone just said. Sometimes I’ll hold my hands up like I’m presenting something. Afterward I know it didn’t make sense for the situation, and it’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing those unusual actions, like arranging pens or repeating phrases, as a direct reaction to what the voices are saying?\n\nPatient: No, not really as a direct reaction to the voices. It just sort of happens. Like in meetings or at gallery events, I might suddenly stand up, move pens or name cards into patterns, repeat something someone just said, or hold my hands up like I’m presenting. Afterward I can tell it didn’t make sense for the room, and I feel embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your actions of arranging pens or repeating phrases are related to other behaviors you exhibit when you're not hearing voices?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they’re part of the same kind of thing. It’s not just the pens or repeating words. Sometimes in meetings or gallery events I’ll suddenly stand up, or move name cards into patterns, or hold my hands up like I’m presenting something. At the time it just happens, and afterward I realize it didn’t fit what was going on and it’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to accomplish something specific when you arrange pens or repeat phrases?\n\nPatient: Not really. It doesn’t feel like I’m trying to get something done. I’ll just suddenly stand up in a meeting, or start arranging pens or name cards into some pattern, or repeat a phrase someone just said. Sometimes I hold my hands up like I’m presenting something. Afterward, I know it didn’t make any sense for the room, and I feel embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like arranging pens or repeating phrases achieves something for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it really achieves anything. It’s not like I feel better afterward or like there was a purpose. I’ll just suddenly do it—stand up, rearrange pens or name cards, repeat what someone said, or hold my hands up like I’m presenting. Then afterward I realize it made no sense in that situation, and I feel embarrassed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It seems to happen when I’m hearing the voices or when I’m really caught up in them. Like in a meeting or at a gallery event, I’ll suddenly stand up and start rearranging pens or name cards into patterns, or I’ll repeat something someone just said. Sometimes I’ll put my hands up like I’m presenting, even though afterward I know it didn’t fit what was happening in the room.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not logical or are disconnected from each other when you're not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that happens. When I’m not hearing anything, my thoughts feel pretty normal to me. I might be tired or distracted, but I don’t usually feel like they’re disconnected or not logical.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the things the voices say about you are true?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. When it’s happening, it can feel true, or at least hard not to believe. Afterward, I’m less sure. I try to tell myself it might just be the voices, but in the moment it gets confusing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you hear are trying to tell you something true about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: I think so, at least when it’s happening. It sounds like board members or staff talking about me through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off. They’ll say I’m ruining the company, and it sounds so clear that I sometimes answer them before I realize no one else heard anything.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voices, how long does it take you to respond or react to what they're saying?\n\nPatient: Usually pretty fast. If it sounds like they’re talking about me through the conference room wall or even from my phone, I’ll answer almost right away—sometimes before I really stop and think. Then I realize no one else heard it, and I feel embarrassed or confused.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific, like rearranging pens or repeating phrases?\n\nPatient: Not really like rearranging pens or repeating phrases. It’s more that I hear people talking about me—like board members or staff through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off—saying I’m ruining the company. It sounds so clear that I answer them before I realize nobody else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble making sense or feel like your thoughts are jumbled when you're talking to others and not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed my thoughts getting jumbled when I’m just talking to someone normally. If that happens, I don’t always catch it myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing those unusual actions, like arranging pens or repeating phrases, because the voices are telling you to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s because the voices tell me to do it. It’s more like, in meetings or gallery events, I’ll suddenly stand up or start moving pens or name cards into patterns, or I’ll repeat something someone just said. Sometimes I’ll hold my hands up like I’m presenting something. Afterward I know it didn’t make sense for the situation, and it’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing those unusual actions, like arranging pens or repeating phrases, as a direct reaction to what the voices are saying?\n\nPatient: No, not really as a direct reaction to the voices. It just sort of happens. Like in meetings or at gallery events, I might suddenly stand up, move pens or name cards into patterns, repeat something someone just said, or hold my hands up like I’m presenting. Afterward I can tell it didn’t make sense for the room, and I feel embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your actions of arranging pens or repeating phrases are related to other behaviors you exhibit when you're not hearing voices?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they’re part of the same kind of thing. It’s not just the pens or repeating words. Sometimes in meetings or gallery events I’ll suddenly stand up, or move name cards into patterns, or hold my hands up like I’m presenting something. At the time it just happens, and afterward I realize it didn’t fit what was going on and it’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to accomplish something specific when you arrange pens or repeat phrases?\n\nPatient: Not really. It doesn’t feel like I’m trying to get something done. I’ll just suddenly stand up in a meeting, or start arranging pens or name cards into some pattern, or repeat a phrase someone just said. Sometimes I hold my hands up like I’m presenting something. Afterward, I know it didn’t make any sense for the room, and I feel embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like arranging pens or repeating phrases achieves something for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it really achieves anything. It’s not like I feel better afterward or like there was a purpose. I’ll just suddenly do it—stand up, rearrange pens or name cards, repeat what someone said, or hold my hands up like I’m presenting. Then afterward I realize it made no sense in that situation, and I feel embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are ever hard to follow or make sense when you're not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. Sometimes I feel a bit scattered, but I don’t know if my thoughts actually don’t make sense when the voices aren’t there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
